\section*{Introduction}

This document aims to present the main results of the project on \emph{Feature Selection} (FS), which is an optimization problem. We are indeed considering a set of variables and we aim to select the most relevant in a specific subset. This feature selection can be very useful to reduce data processing, to detect the core characteristics of a system or to train efficiently a model. In fact, we have two different objectives: we want to select as few features as possible and we want to have the more accurate model for our characteristics.

Some optimization problems are so hard that we can't find any algorithm, \textit{i.e.} a finite sequence of instructions in order to get the best solution, to solve it. In complexity theory, we can define an NP problem by two properties: we can easily verify a solution -- polynomial complexity -- but we cannot design a process to build an efficient solution. The FS is an example of NP-problem, so we need to explore different approaches to find the best solution possible. As it is a two-objectives problem, we will first map it into a single objective binary problem. By doing so, we will be able to implement and compare single-objective optimizers such as \emph{Differential Evolution} (DE), \emph{Simulated Annealing} (SA) and \emph{Particle Swarm Optimization} (PSO). %We will finally compare our results with a multi-objective optimizers \emph{Non-Domintaed Sorting Genetic Algorithm 2} (NSGA-II).

In order to run our numerical experiments, we will use several datasets with different number of features and of instances. In that way, we will discuss and compare the performance or each method regarding the size of the considered problem.

We will start by presenting the \emph{feature selection problem} and presenting our datasets \ref{sec:fs}. Then, we will provide some theoretical review on formalizing an optimization problem and on effective methods to solve it \ref{sec:theoretical_review}. We will also present our implementation and protocol for our numerical analysis \ref{sec:implementation_protocol}. Finally, we will present our method to determine the best set of parameters for our algorithms \ref{sec:finding_optimal_parameters} and, to finish, we will present our results and analysis to the given problem \ref{sec:results_analysis}.

\section{The Feature Selection Problem}\label{sec:fs}

This section aims to present extensively our problem. We will provide a general overview \ref{sec:general_overview}, then we will describe our objectives \ref{sec:objectives}. The chosen problem representation is then explained \ref{sec:chosen_problem_representation} before mentioning the specifics of our problem \ref{sec:specifics} and our specific constraints \ref{sec:constraints}. Finally, we will describe the datasets used to run our numerical experiments \ref{sec:datasets}.

\subsection{General overview}\label{sec:general_overview}

Let's consider a set of features $\mathcal{S}$ that describes a complex systems with multiple characteristics. The \emph{feature selection} is a process designed to select the minimal subset $\mathcal{T} \subset \mathcal{S}$ that contains relevant features to describe a specific characteristic.

Hence, this is a multi-objective problem as we aim to to maximize the performance of our model and reduce the number of features used to describe our characteristic.

In order to map the 2-objective problem into a single-objective problem, we can use a weight $\alpha \in \lbrack 0,1 \rbrack$ so that we can define our fitness as following:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:objective_function_old}
    f(x) = \alpha E(x) + (1-\alpha) \frac{\abs{\mathcal{T}}}{\abs{\mathcal{S}}},
\end{equation}

where $E(x)$ is the classification error, $\abs{\mathcal{T}}$ the number of selected features and $\abs{\mathcal{S}}$ the total number of features.

Although equation \eqref{eq:objective_function_old} provides a standard theoretical framework to balance both objectives, evaluating the fitness solely based on the model's pure classification accuracy:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:objective_function}
    f(x) = \text{Accuracy}
\end{equation}

proved to be a better approach for our numerical experiments. This design choice was driven by several practical and analytical considerations. First, appropriately tuning the hyperparameter $\alpha$ across different datasets and algorithms is highly impractical and introduces an arbitrary bias. More importantly, aggregating both the error and the feature ratio into a single metric obscures the model's inherent sensitivity to the dimensionality of the data. Different evaluators (such as SVM or $k-$NN) inherently favor different feature subset sizes and characteristics. By relying exclusively on accuracy as the fitness function, we can draw clearer, unbiased conclusions regarding the feature sets naturally preferred by each model. Finally, since adding irrelevant or redundant features typically degrades a model's accuracy due to the curse of dimensionality, an explicit penalty term for the number of selected features was ultimately deemed unnecessary.

\subsection{Objectives}\label{sec:objectives}

Our main goal is to determine the best binary sequence $s^*$ that minimize the number of selected features and maximize the accuracy of the model so that minimize the objective function defined in \eqref{eq:objective_function} using a weight to map the two-objectives problem into a single-objective problem.

In order to achieve that, our goal is to describe, implement and compare several methods (\tableref{methods_objectives}) : DE, SA and PSO.

Finally, this comparison and the analysis will let us determine the best subset of features for each method and in which situation each method is better.

        \textbf{Differential Evolution (DE)} & \cite{storn1997differential}, \cite{przewozniczek_de}, \cite{1688535} & The differential evolution is a metaheuristics which is a part of evolutionary algorithm: it aims to improve step by step a candidate solution within a population using an approach base on the difference of several vectors which are individuals in the population. \subsection{Chosen problem representation}\label{sec:chosen_problem_representation}

For a given set of features $\mathcal{S}$, we will write a binary sequence $s$ of size $\abs{\mathcal{S}}$. For each bit on position $i$, if $s(i)=1$, then the $i-$th feature is selected. Otherwise, if $s(i)=0$, the $i-$the feature is not selected.


\subsection{Specifics}\label{sec:specifics}

The FS problem has different core characteristics and specifics that motive us to use certain type of optimizers.

First, as illustrated in subsection \ref{sec:chosen_problem_representation}, each feature can either be selected or discard. Therefore the search space contains $2^{\abs{\mathcal{S}}}$ possible combinations and it is a \emph{combinatorial} search space. $2^{\abs{\mathcal{S}}}$ is in fact the cardinal of all parties of the set $\mathcal{S}$. We can observe, hence, that this is an exponential growth that makes exhaustive search impossible and explains the need of metaheuristics.

Then, we need to specify our fitness function shortly presented in \eqref{eq:objective_function} as it is based on another function which is the classification error $E(x)$. To compute this error, a machine learning classifier (described in subsection \ref{sec:ligh_model}) must be trained and evaluated using cross-validation on the selected subset $\mathcal{T}$ for every candidate solution. Therefor, this evaluation step is computationally expensive.

Finally, most of our optimizers (\tableref{methods_objectives}) are commonly defined in continuous search space (see for instance \cite{przewozniczek_ga_basics}, \cite{przewozniczek_de}, \cite{przewozniczek_pso}) so we need to map the real value $x_i$ to binary value $x_i^{\text{bit}}$. We will discuss this mapping for each optimizer in section \ref{sec:theoretical_review} but this can be generally done using a sigmoid function (see \eqref{eq:sigmoid} and \figref{sigmoid}).

\subsection{Constraints}\label{sec:constraints}

To ensure that the solutions generated by our algorithms are valid and evaluable, we need to handle non-empty subset and to use a strict binary sequence.

First, a machine learning model cannot be trained without any input data. Therefore, a candidate solution cannot be a zero-vector. It means that we must select at least one feature. Hence, by writing $s(i)$ the $i-$th value of the binary sequence $s$, we have the following constraint

\begin{equation}\label{eq:non_zero_constraint}
    \sum_{i=1}^{\abs{\mathcal{S}}} s(i) \geq 1
\end{equation}

If an optimizer generates a totally empty subset (all zeros), a penalty must be applied to its fitness score, or the solution must be fixed so that we can compute the fitness value. We can for example mention randomly flipping one bit to $1$ before evaluation.
    
Then, the final decoded solution used to mask the dataset must consist strictly of boolean values, meaning $s(i) \in \{0, 1\}$ for all $i \in \{1, \dots, \abs{\mathcal{S}}\}$. Any other continuous values generated by the algorithms must be strictly converted before the evaluation of $E(x)$ to make our approach consistent.

\section{Theoretical review}\label{sec:theoretical_review}

\subsection{Solving an Optimization problem : Exploration and Exploitation}\label{sec:optimization_problem_exploration_exploitation}

When it comes to find an optimal solution to a given problem, we can first try to describe well our problem. They are two main categories of problem : \emph{combinatorial} and \emph{topological}. In optimization, a \emph{topological} problem seeks the best continuous distribution of material within a physical space (such as designing the most efficient shape for a mechanical component), whereas a \emph{combinatorial} problem involves finding the optimal discrete arrangement from a finite set of possibilities (such as finding the best sequence of tasks in the PFSP or the best binary sequence for the \textit{feature selection} problem).

A general $n-$dimensional optimization problem is described by an objective function $f$

\begin{equation}
    f:\mathcal{D}_f \subset \mathbb{R}^n \to R ,
\end{equation}

where $\mathcal{D}_f$ is the solution search space. Our objective is therefore to find $x^*$ which can be the maximum or the minimum of $f$, let's assume we are looking for a maximum (even though it means changing $f$ to $-f$).

\begin{equation}
    x^* = \underset{x \in \mathcal{D}_x \subset \mathcal{D}_f}{\arg \max} f(x),
\end{equation}

where $\mathcal{D}_x$ is the feasible set.

In the \textit{feature selection} problem, the objective function is defined \eqref{eq:objective_function} and we can observe that finding the maximum of this function is really hard because this function is not linear, nor convex. Therefore, as it is a NP-hard problem, we cannot use algorithms to solve this problem. Instead, we will use several methods to look for the best solution possible in the feasible set. We will now present these approaches: the Differential Evolution \ref{sec:DE_theory_principle}, the Simulated Annealing \ref{sec:simulated_annealing_theoretical_reviews} and the Particle Swarm Optimization approach \ref{sec:PSO_theory}.

\subsection{Differential Evolution}\label{sec:DE_theory}

\subsubsection{Principle}\label{sec:DE_theory_principle}

\emph{Differential Evolution} (DE) is a \emph{metaheuristics}, a particular case of \emph{evolutionary algorithm}, design to solve an optimization problem by improving step-by-step a candidate solution in regards to a specific measure (fitness function). This method was originally proposed by R. Storn and K. Price in 1997 (see \cite{storn1997differential}).

At origin, this method was designed for \emph{topological} problem, using therefore a continuous search space. However, there are some alterative version of DE to handle \emph{combinatorial} problem such as binary problem (\ref{sec:bde}).

As the DE is based on EA, it is based on classical crossover and on typical search mechanisms for continuous search spaces.

Let's now present in details the main mechanism for DE as presented in \cite{przewozniczek_de}. The main loop starts by initializing a population. Then, until a specific stop condition is met, we will for each individual \texttt{ind}:

\begin{itemize}
    \item create a candidate solution \texttt{indCand};
    \item if the fitness of \texttt{ind} is less or equal than the fitness of \texttt{indCand}, we replace the \texttt{ind} by \texttt{indCand}.
\end{itemize}

We now need to precise how to create the candidate individual. In order to do that, we need to randomly pick 3 individuals of the population: \texttt{indBase}, \texttt{ind0} and \texttt{ind1}. Then, we will create the candidate individual if all four individuals differs.

In that case, for each gene, we will create a \texttt{mutantInd} with the following relation:

\begin{equation}
    \small \texttt{mutantInd}[\texttt{geneOff}] \leftarrow \texttt{indBase}[\texttt{geneOff}] + F \cdot \left(\texttt{ind0}[\texttt{geneOff}] - \texttt{ind1}[\texttt{geneOff}]\right),
\end{equation}

where $F$ represents the \emph{scaling factor} or the \emph{differential weight}, which is one of the core parameters of that method. Usually, $F\in \lbrack 0,2\rbrack$ as describe in \cite{przewozniczek_de}. In order to create finally the offspring, we need to use another parameter $P_x$ the \emph{crossover probability}.

In order to generate each gene of the new individual, we will randomly choose a real number between $0$ and $1$ and there is now two cases:

\begin{itemize}
    \item if that number is less that the \emph{crossover probability}, we define the gene as as the mutant gene previously generated;
    \item otherwise, the gene is defined by the \texttt{ind}.
\end{itemize}

To put it in a nutshell, the candidate solution is generated in a way to preserve the original genotype and to have a mutant part base on crossover on the difference of the two others individuals. In that way, DE uses both properties of local search and \emph{Genetic Algorithms} (GA) (see a description in \textsc{Appendices}).

Finally, there is multiple way to define the base vector: we can for instance choose randomly the individual or the best one. We can also apply the same scheme with more than two individuals to make the mutation. Then, we can also define several crossover types such as the binomial (bin) and exponential (exp). This leads us to the DE-naming convention as follows: \textbf{DE $x$/$y$/$z$}, where $x$ designate the way of choosing the base vector, $y$ the number of different vectors used by mutation and $z$ the crossover type, where a vector is the difference of two different individuals.

If we use multiple vectors, we need to define the same amount of scaling factors $F_i$ where $i$ is the for the $i-$th vector. For instance, if we consider DE best/2/bin, we use two different vectors between four individuals $X_1, X_2,X_3$ and $X_4$. As we use the best method to select the base individual, the mutated solution would be:

\begin{equation*}
    X = X_{\text{best}} + F_1 \cdot (X_1 - X_2) + F_2\cdot (X_3 - X_4)
\end{equation*}

\subsubsection{Variants to solve binary problem}\label{sec:bde}

\subsubsubsection{Binary Differential Evolution (BDE)}

As detailed in \ref{sec:general_overview}, the feature selection problem is a \emph{combinatorial} problem so it is not living in a continuous search space. Therefore, the usual DE cannot be applied to our problem. However, we can adapt the DE to map a binary problem.

In order to transform the continuous value to probability, we can use \emph{transfer function} or \emph{squashing function}. A well-known example of this type of function is the logistic function sigmoid:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:sigmoid}
    \begin{array}{r|rcl}
    S: & \mathbb{R} & \to     & \lbrack 0,1\rbrack \\
       & x          & \mapsto & \frac{1}{1+e^{-x}}
    \end{array}
\end{equation}

As $e^{-x}\geq0$ for all real $x$, that $e^{-x} \underset{x\to +\infty}{\longrightarrow} 0$ and $e^{-x} \underset{x\to -\infty}{\longrightarrow} +\infty$, we can easily show that $S(x) \in \lbrack0,1\rbrack$, as illustrated on \figref{sigmoid}. Therefore, $S(x)$ can represent a probability.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{img/sigmoid.png}
    \caption{\textbf{Illustration of the sigmoid function for $x\in \lbrack -20,20\rbrack$.} We can see how this function transform $x\in \mathbb{R}$ into $S(x) \in \lbrack0,1\rbrack$ (the dotted line are the two horizontal tangent).}
    \label{fig:sigmoid}
\end{figure}

In practice, we use the continuous version of the DE and we will get a vector value $x\in \mathbb{R}$. We will then compute $S(x)$ which will represent the probability to get $1$. Once $S(x)$ computed, we will randomly generate a number between $0$ and $1$, noted $p$. If $p<S(x)$, it represents a "$1$". Otherwise, if $p\geq S(x)$, it represents a "$0$".

In that way, we are able to map a continuous value into a binary one. However, we can note that the size of the problem is the same as we don't change the dimension of the problem: if we have $n$ real value, we will have a sequence of $n$ bits.

\subsubsubsection{Angle Modulation Differential Evolution (AMDE)}

Another variant of the DE called "Angle Modulation Differential Evolution" (AMDE) was proposed in 2006 and can deal with binary problem (see \cite{1688535}).

Instead of directly working with a binary sequence, they suggested to use a continuous function to generate this sequence. This is a trigonometry-based function defined as following:

\begin{equation}
    g(x) = \sin(2\pi(x-a) \times b \times \cos(A))+d, \qquad \text{where } A=2\pi \times c(x-a).
\end{equation}

In this function, $a$ represents the horizontal shift of the generating function, $b$ the maximum frequency of the composed $\sin$ function, $c$ the frequency of the composed $\cos$ function and $d$ determines the vertical shift of the generating function. The representation of theses coefficients can be illustrated with the \figref{g_x_illustration}.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=.7\linewidth]{img/g_x_illustration.png}
    \caption{\textbf{Bit string generating function with default values $a = 0, b =1, c = 1, d = 0$.} Source: \cite{1688535}}
    \label{fig:g_x_illustration}
\end{figure}

In order to create a binary sequence of $n$ bits, the algorithm first choose $n$ uniformly spaced $x$ and we compute $g(x)$. If $g(x)>0$, the bit is $1$. If $g(x) \leq 0$, the bit is 0.

In fact, if we want to solve a binary problem with $n$ bits, the AMDE just needs to optimize the four parameters $a,b,c,d$ so it is well-designed to reduce computation time and iterations.

The \myalgref{amde} presents how this is effectively done in a traditional loop of evolution.

\begin{algorithm}
\caption{AMDE Algorithm}
\label{alg:amde}
\begin{algorithmic}
\State Initalize a population and set control parameter values
\Repeat
    \State Select required individuals for reproduction scheme
    \State Produce an offspring individual
    \State Evaluate the fitness of the offspring individual by generating bit string and passing to fitness function
    \If{$F_{AMDE}(offspring) <= F_{AMDE}(parent)$}
        \State Replace parent individual with offspring
    \Else
        \State Retain parent individual
    \EndIf
\Until{Stopping condition is met}
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsection{Simulated Annealing}\label{sec:simulated_annealing_theoretical_reviews}

\subsubsection{Overview}

The \emph{simulated annealing} is based on the annealing's process in metallurgy. In this process, we aim to create a more solid material by alternating slow cooling and heating (annealing) in order to relax the micro-fissure within the material and, thus, going in a more stable form of the material, and therefore minimize the total energy of the material.

Therefore, by analogy, the algorithm's process can be described by : first, we choose a random initial state in the feasible set. This state is characterized by its total energy $E_0$ (which is in fact evaluated by the objective function) and we use the fictive parameter $T$ as for the temperature in the real annealing. At each step, we change slightly the state (in the \emph{neighborhood}) and we get therefore a difference of energy $\Delta E$. If $\Delta E<0$, then the system has less energy so is more stable so we get that change. Otherwise, if $\Delta E > 0$, then the neighbor is worse but we still accept it with a probability $\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta E}{T}\right)$. Then, we continue to iterate until we reach the maximal number of iterations or if we obtain a pretty good solution.

We now need to describe how the temperature will change, we can set it piecewise constant or following a law of evolution. The most common is the geometric one \cite{wiki_recuit_simule_fr}, we can therefore write:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:rec_sa}
    T_{i+1} = \lambda T_i,
\end{equation}

where $\lambda < 1$ (otherwise it is constant) and we usually take $\lambda = 0,99$ \cite{kirkpatrick1983optimization}.

This method has the advantage that, under some hypothesis, we can prove that this process will converge in probability \cite{lacroix2001markov}. But the huge disadvantage of this approach is that it is very hard to determine the good parameters to fit the model.

In fact, the temperature has a huge impact: with high temperature, the system can easily move in the feasible set because $\exp\left(-\frac{\Delta E}{T}\right)$ is close to $1$. However, with low temperature, the changes that decrease the energy of the system are accepted but others are also accepted which allow us not to be blocked inside a local minimum.

\subsubsection{Adaptation for the feature selection problem}

The part of the simulated annealing that depends on the considered problem is the generation of the neighbourhood. For a permutation method, we can implement a swap approach but for the feature selection, as it is a binary problem, we can just do a bit flip.
\begin{algorithm}[H]
\caption{Simulated Annealing for Feature Selection}
\label{alg:sa_fs}
\begin{algorithmic}[1]
\Require $N$ (number of features), $eval_{\max}$ (maximum evaluations), $T_0$ (initial temperature), $\alpha$ (cooling rate)
\Ensure $g$ (best solution found)

\State $s \gets \text{Random binary vector of size } N$ \textit{// Current solution}
\State $\text{Evaluate}(s)$
\State $g \gets s$ \textit{// Global best solution}
\State $T \gets T_0$ \textit{// Set up initial temperature}

\For{$i \gets 1$ \textbf{to} $eval_{\max} - 1$}
    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \State \textit{// Generate neighbor (Bit Flip)}
    \State $s_n \gets \text{Copy}(s)$
    \State $idx \gets \text{RandomInteger}(0, N - 1)$
    \State $s_n[idx] \gets 1 - s_n[idx]$
    \State $\text{Evaluate}(s_n)$
    
    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \State $\Delta f \gets f(s_n) - f(s)$ \textit{// Compute fitness difference}
    
    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \State \textit{// Acceptance criterion}
    \If{$\Delta f < 0$} \textit{// The neighbor is better}
        \State $s \gets s_n$
        \vspace{0.1cm}
        \State \textit{// Update global best}
        \If{$f(s_n) < f(g)$}
            \State $g \gets \text{Copy}(s_n)$
        \EndIf
    \Else \textit{// The neighbor is worse, accept with a probability}
        \State $p \gets \exp(-\Delta f / T)$
        \If{$\text{Random}(0, 1) < p$}
            \State $s \gets s_n$
        \EndIf
    \EndIf
    
    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \State \textit{// Cooling down}
    \State $T \gets T \times \alpha$
    
    \vspace{0.1cm}
    \State \textit{// Prevent division by zero / machine precision issue}
    \If{$T < 10^{-10}$}
        \State $T \gets 10^{-10}$
    \EndIf
\EndFor

\State \Return $g$
\end{algorithmic}
\end{algorithm}

\subsection{Particle Swarm Optimization}\label{sec:PSO_theory}

\subsubsection{Principle}

As its name suggests, the \emph{Particle Swarm Optimization} (PSO) was inspired by animal swarms. In this swarm, we have a leader that provide the genereal direction toward each bird will follow. However, each bird is free to make its own decisions. Initially, this method was proposed for continuous search spaces and is very relevant to deal with noised problem or multiple optima \cite{kennedy1995particle}.

Each bird of the swarm can be characterized by its position $x_{i,j}(t)$ where $i$ is the number of the bird, $j$ the dimension, $t$ the iteration illustrated by the time and its speed and move direction $v_{i,j}(t)$. The position of the next iteration is the sum of the previous position and the move direction (see \cite{przewozniczek_pso}).

\begin{equation}\label{eq:position_pso}
    x_{i,j}(t+1)=x_{i,j}(t) + v_{i,j}(t+1)
\end{equation}

We now to study how this speed $v_{i,j}(t)$ evolve throughout iterations. A common description is detailed in \cite{vandenbergh2006study} where we have

\begin{equation}\label{eq:velocity_pso}
    v_{i,j}(t+1) = \lambda v_{i,j}(t) + c_pr_p \left(x_{i,j}^{\text{best}} - x_{i,j}(t)\right) + c_g r_g \left(g_{j}^{\text{best}} - x_{i,j}(t)\right).
\end{equation}

In this equation, $\lambda$ is an inertia coefficient, $c_p$ and $c_g$ are coefficient between $0$ and $2$, $r_p$ and $r_g$ are two random numbers between $0$ and $1$, $x_{i,j}^{\text{best}}$ is the $j-$th dimension of the best solution of the $i-$th bird and, in the same way, $g_{j}^{\text{best}}$ is the $j-$th dimension of the best solution of the whole swarm. In fact, $c_pr_p$ represent the cognitive or personal component and $c_g r_g$ the social or group component.

The value of these coefficients are discussed in \cite{vandenbergh2006study}. In fact, we can represent $\lambda$ regarding $\phi_1+\phi_2$, where $\phi_1=c_p r_p$ and $\phi_2=c_g r_g$.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{Main_Project/img/pso_coefficients_values.png}
    \caption{\textbf{The black triangle to the bottom right represents the values for which the particle strictly diverges, i.e. $\max\left(\norm{\lambda_1}, \norm{\lambda_2}\right) > 1$. This is the region for which $w < 0.5(\phi_1 + \phi_2)-1$. The lighter regions represent the magnitude of $\max\left(\norm{\lambda_1}, \norm{\lambda_2}\right)$, with white representing magnitude $1$. The darker regions (outside of the divergent region) represent values leading to more rapid convergence.} Source: \cite{vandenbergh2006study}}
    \label{fig:placeholder}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection{Variants to solve binary problem}\label{sec:binary_PSO}

\subsubsubsection{Binary Particle Swarm Optimization}

\paragraph{BPSO by Kennedy and Eberhart} In order to handle binary search space, Kennedy and Eberhart in \cite{kennedy1997discrete} had to redefine the meaning of the velocity. In the continuous search spaces, the velocity represents the physical movement of bird but, in the binary search spaces, it represents the probability that a specific component of the binary sequence takes the value "$1$".

The velocity update equation \eqref{eq:velocity_pso} remains structurally identical to the continuous version. To map this continuous velocity $v_{i,j}(t+1)$ to a valid probability range $[0, 1]$, a logistic sigmoid function is applied (see \eqref{eq:sigmoid} and \figref{sigmoid}):

\begin{equation}
    S\left(v_{i,j}(t+1)\right) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-v_{i,j}(t+1)}}
\end{equation}

Similarly to the BDE, the position update rule is then replaced by a probabilistic decision based on a random number $r_{i,j}$ drawn from a uniform distribution in $[0, 1]$:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:decision_BPSO}
    x_{i,j}(t+1) = 
    \begin{cases} 
        1 & \text{if } r_{i,j} < S(v_{i,j}(t+1)) \\
        0 & \text{otherwise}
    \end{cases}
\end{equation}

To prevent the search process from stagnating, it is crucial to avoid probabilities becoming exactly $0$ or $1$. Therefore, it is a common practice introduced in the original paper to limit the velocity within a range $[-V_{\max}, V_{\max}]$. A typical threshold is $V_{\max} = 4$, which bounds the maximum probability of changing a bit's state between approximately $0.018$ and $0.982$.

\paragraph{Main problems with binary PSO}

According to \cite{4433821}, there are two main problems and concerns about binary
PSO: parameters and memory.

While the BPSO of Kennedy and Eberhart is commonly implemented, some major differences were observed in the core mechanism of PSO (for continuous search space) and BPSO (for binary search space).

For instance, in the PSO, if there is no velocity ($\forall t \in \mathbb{N}, v_{i,j}(t)=0$), then there is no movement using relation \eqref{eq:position_pso} ($\forall t \in \mathbb{N}, x_{i,j}(t+1)=x_{i,j}(t)$). Therefore, in PSO, a zero velocity implies that the bird is stationary. However, in BPSO, if we have $v=0$, then we get a probability to get "$1$" of

\begin{equation*}
    S(0)=\frac{1}{1+e^{-0}} = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5.
\end{equation*}

Hence, the bird has $50\%$ chance to take the value "$1$" (or "$0$" in fact). And that is actually a core paradox between these two implementations: for the PSO, $v=0$ implies no change and for the BPSO $v=0$ implies a maximum randomness and changes.

Furthermore, using \eqref{eq:decision_BPSO}, we can observe that the next value for the bit is
almost independent of the current value of that bit and the value is solely updated using the velocity vector. But in standard continuous PSO, the update rule for the position uses current position and the velocity vector (see \eqref{eq:velocity_pso}).

\paragraph{NBPSO by Khanesar, Teshnehlab, and Shoorehdeli}

In order to resolve the inconsistencies between PSO and BPSO, Khanesar, Teshnehlab, and Shoorehdeli proposed a \emph{Novel Binary Particle Swar Optimization} (NBPSO) in 2007 (see \cite{4433821}). The core contribution of their approach is a complete redefinition of the velocity's meaning to align it with the continuous version.

In NBPSO, the velocity of a particle is defined as the \emph{probability of changing its state} from its previous value to its complement, rather than the absolute probability of the bit being $1$. This redefinition restores the original physical meaning of velocity as a "rate of change". Consequently, if a particle's velocity drops to zero in NBPSO, its probability of changing state is zero. The particle remains stationary, as it was the case with the continuous PSO.

To implement this dynamic, Khanesar et al. introduced two separate velocity vectors for each particle:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $v_{i,j}^1$ which is the probability that the $j$-th bit changes to $1$ (applied if the current bit is $0$) ;
    \item $v_{i,j}^0$ which is the probability that the $j$-th bit changes to $0$ (applied if the current bit is $1$).
\end{itemize}

These velocities are updated based on whether the particle's current bit matches its personal best ($x_{i,j}^{\text{best}}$) or the global best ($g_j^{\text{best}}$). If the current bit differs from these best-known positions, the respective velocity to flip toward that better state is increased using the standard cognitive and social components.

By interpreting velocity as a mutation rate rather than an absolute state probability, the NBPSO allows the standard PSO parameters (such as the inertia weight) to behave exactly as they do in the continuous domain. This leads to more stable exploitation of the search space and prevents the purely random search behavior associated with low velocities in the original BPSO.

\subsection{Comparison between parameters}\label{sec:friedman}

\subsubsection{Friedman test}

To decide which set of parameters performs best, we cannot simply pick the highest mean accuracy because, after getting results from the experiments, we observe that the differences are very small ($\sim 1\%$) and the ranking varies across datasets and models.

Therefore, we use the non-parametric procedure recommended for comparing several configurations over multiple datasets~\cite{demsar2006statistical}. The $k$ different tested configurations are treated as the \emph{treatments} and each (dataset, model) pair is treated as an independent \emph{block}; with four datasets and two evaluators (SVM and kNN) this leads to $N=8$ blocks.

Within every block, the $k$ configurations are ranked by their mean accuracy over the ten runs (rank $1$ = best, average ranks in case of ties).

Let $R_j$ denote the average rank of ratio $j$ over the $N$ blocks. The Friedman statistic is

\begin{equation}\label{eq:friedman}
    \chi_F^2 = \frac{12N}{k(k+1)}\left[\sum_{j=1}^{k} R_j^2 - \frac{k(k+1)^2}{4}\right],
\end{equation}

computed with the usual correction for tied ranks.

If the $p-$value of this tests is less than $0.05$, it means that the null hypothesis is rejected and, thus, that there is indeed a significant difference. In that case, we will then proceed with the Post-Hoc test to determine which configuration is indeed the best.

Otherwise, if the $p-$value is greater than $0.05$, there is no significant difference between the different configurations.

Because $N$ is small, we also report the Iman--Davenport statistic, which is less conservative and follows an $F$ distribution~\cite{iman1980approximations}:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:iman_davenport}
    F_F = \frac{(N-1)\,\chi_F^2}{N(k-1)-\chi_F^2} \sim F\big(k-1,\,(k-1)(N-1)\big).
\end{equation}

\subsubsection{Nemenyi Post-Hoc test}
When the null hypothesis (all ratios equivalent) is rejected, we apply the Nemenyi post-hoc test: two ratios are significantly different if their average ranks differ by at least the critical difference

\begin{equation}\label{eq:nemenyi_cd}
    CD = q_\alpha \sqrt{\frac{k(k+1)}{6N}},
\end{equation}

where $q_\alpha$ is the critical value of the Studentized range statistic ($q_{0.05}=2.569$ for $k=4$ for example).

\section{Implementation and protocol}\label{sec:implementation_protocol}


\subsection{Description of the protocol}

This section will describe the numerical protocol that we apply to compare efficiently and in a fair play way all our methods to solve the PFSP problem. First, we will describe the environment and the hardware used \ref{sec:hardware_environment}. Then, we will precise the stop condition that we used \ref{sec:stop_condition} and how we measured computation time \ref{sec:computation_time}. 

\subsubsection{Hardware description and environment}\label{sec:hardware_environment}

\paragraph{Computer used to run the tests} 

All numerical experiments were executed locally on a single machine to ensure a fair and consistent evaluation environment. The hardware configuration of the computer used for these tests is detailed as follows:

\paragraph{Source code}

\subsection{Main structure}

According to the pseudo-code presented in section \ref{sec:theoretical_review}, we have implemented these methods using the Object-oriented programming. All the code and the files of the project is available here:

\href{https://github.com/MaximeLuce/Feature-Selection-solving-the-problem-and-application-to-signal-processing}{\small https://github.com/MaximeLuce/Feature-Selection-solving-the-problem-and-application-to-signal-processing}.


\subsubsection{Stop condition}\label{sec:stop_condition}

To ensure a strictly fair and unbiased comparison between the different stochastic algorithms (Random Search, Evolutionary Algorithm, and Simulated Annealing), we established a common computational budget rather than relying on a time-based limit. The stop condition was strictly defined by a maximum Number of Function Evaluations (NFE) set to 10 000. 
 

\subsubsection{Measuring computation time}\label{sec:computation_time}

It is also useful to accurately measure the real-world execution time in order to evaluate the practical efficiency of each algorithm.

To measure the elapsed time in our Python implementation, we use the \texttt{timeit} module instead of the most common \texttt{time} as \texttt{timeit} is much more accurate \cite{so_time_vs_timeit_2013}. As widely documented in Python benchmarking standards, \texttt{time.time()} measures the real-world wall-clock time and is susceptible to system clock updates, which can lead to inaccuracies during very short executions.

\section{Finding the optimal parameters}\label{sec:finding_optimal_parameters}

As described in \tableref{methods_objectives}, we will use four methods that require parameters : the Differential Evolution, the Simulated Annealing and the Particle Swarm Optimization. To compare all these methods, we first need to find the optimal parameters.

First, we present all the cases used to determine the best set of parameters for these methods \ref{sec:description_cases}. Then, we run and analyze experiment for each case \ref{sec:running_experiment} and we summarize all the best set of parameters found in \ref{sec:summary_parameters}.

\subsection{Description of the different cases}\label{sec:description_cases}

We will first present the cases in order to determine the best set of parameters for the differential evolution \ref{sec:cases_de}: both binary differential evolution \ref{sec:cases_bde} and angle module differential evolution \ref{sec:cases_amde}.

Then, we will present the cases for the Simulated Annealing including initial temperature and cooling rate parameters \ref{sec:cases_sa}.

Finally, we will describe the different cases used for the novel binary swarm optimization \ref{sec:cases_nbpso}.

\subsubsection{Differential Evolution}\label{sec:cases_de}

\paragraph{Overview of parameters}

The Differential Evolution relies on five main parameters, as described in section \ref{sec:DE_theory_principle}: the strategy to choose the base vector, the population size ($NP$), the number of generations ($G$), the mutation scaling factor ($F$), and the crossover rate ($CR$).

However, it exists other parameters such as the type of crossover. For our analysis, we fixe this parameter and we use a binomial \texttt{bin} crossover.

Furthermore, for the Binary Differential Evolution, we fixed the transfer function by using sigmoid function (\figref{sigmoid}) and, therefore, we don't take into account in our study the impact of different type of transfer function.


\subsubsubsection{Binary Differential Evolution (BDE)}\label{sec:cases_bde}

\paragraph{Strategy to choose the base vector}
The Differential Evolution algorithm is heavily defined by its mutation strategy, with the naming convention described in section \ref{sec:DE_theory_principle}. The choice of the base vector (random vs. best) and the number of difference vectors ($1$ or $2$) dictates the balance between exploration and exploitation. 

Because the others parameters are highly dependent on the chosen strategy, we must determine the best mutation scheme first (\tableref{Cases_Determine_BDE_Strategy}). To do so, we use standard literature values for the control parameters: $NP=50$, $G=199$ ($9950+50 =10\,000$ evaluations), $F=0.5$, and $CR=0.5$. Note that for strategies involving two difference vectors (\textit{rand/2} and \textit{best/2}), we use a single scaling factor $F$ for both difference operations to constrain the parameter space.

\subsubsubsection{Angle Modulation Differential Evolution (AMDE)}\label{sec:cases_amde}

\paragraph{Overview of parameters} The Angle Modulation approach utilizes a standard continuous Differential Evolution algorithm to optimize a 4-dimensional continuous space (the 4 coefficients $a, b, c, d$ of the trigonometric generating function), regardless of the actual number of features in the dataset.

Because the search space is strictly limited to 4 dimensions, the AMDE requires different parameter tuning than the BDE, especially concerning the population size which can be significantly smaller. We apply the same sequential methodology to find its optimal parameters: $Strat^{**}$, $NP^{**}, G^{**}, F^{**}$, and $CR^{**}$. All coefficients $a,b,c,d$ are initialized in $[-6;+6]$ to avoid divergence as detailed in \cite{1688535}.

First, we study the impact of the strategy to choose the base vector on the performance (\tableref{Cases_Determine_AMDE_Strategy}).

\subsubsection{Simulated Annealing}\label{sec:cases_sa}

We now present the same approach for the simulated annealing method with only two parameters : the initial temperature and the cooling rate. We use common value (see section \ref{sec:simulated_annealing_theoretical_reviews}) to make also a grid search (\tableref{Cases_Determine_SA_Schedule}) in order to find an equilibrium between early \emph{exploration} and convergence speed.

\subsubsection{Novel Binary Particle Swarm Optimization (NBPSO)}\label{sec:cases_nbpso}

As established in section \ref{sec:binary_PSO}, the Novel Binary Particle Swarm Optimization (NBPSO) introduced by Khanesar et al. \cite{4433821} redefines the velocity as the probability of changing a bit's state. Because this restores the physical meaning of velocity from the continuous domain, we can tune the NBPSO using standard continuous PSO parameters: the swarm size ($S$), the number of iterations ($G$), the inertia weight ($\lambda$), the cognitive coefficient ($c_p$), and the social coefficient ($c_g$).

\paragraph{Swarm size/iterations ratio} First, we search for the best trade-off between the swarm size ($S$) and the number of iterations ($G$) (\tableref{Cases_Determine_NBPSO_Pop_Gen}). To maintain a fair comparison with our previous algorithms, the total number of fitness evaluations is fixed to $10\,000$. During this stage, we use standard empirical values for the other parameters: $\lambda=0.729$, and $c_p = c_g = 1.49445$.

\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
        \hline 
        \textbf{Case n°} & \textbf{Swarm Size ($S$)} & \textbf{Iterations ($G$)} & $\lambda$ & $c_p$ & $c_g$ & \textbf{Run per algo} \\ \hline

        \textbf{55} & 100 & 99 & 0.729 & 1.49445 & 1.49445 & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{56} & 50 & 199 & 0.729 & 1.49445 & 1.49445 & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{57} & 20 & 499 & 0.729 & 1.49445 & 1.49445 & 10 \\ 
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{\textbf{Description of the parameters used to determine the best Swarm Size / Iterations ratio for NBPSO.}}
    \label{tab:Cases_Determine_NBPSO_Pop_Gen}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Ratio social and group} Let us assume the best configuration found is denoted by $S^*$ and $G^*$. We then proceed to tune the acceleration coefficients, $c_p$ and $c_g$ (\tableref{Cases_Determine_NBPSO_C1_C2}). These parameters dictate the behavior of the particles: a higher $c_p$ favors personal exploration (cognitive), while a higher $c_g$ accelerates convergence toward the best-known solution in the swarm (social). We test a balanced approach, a cognitive-heavy approach, and a social-heavy approach. These parameters should verify \cite{4433821}:

\begin{equation}\label{eq:pso_convergence_condition}
    \lambda \geq \frac{c_p + c_g}{2} - 1 \quad \Longleftrightarrow \quad c_p + c_g \leq 2(\lambda + 1).
\end{equation}


\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
        \hline 
        \textbf{Case n°} & \textbf{Swarm Size ($S$)} & \textbf{Iterations ($G$)} & $\lambda$ & $c_p$ & $c_g$ & \textbf{Run per algo} \\ \hline

        \textbf{58} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & 0.729 & 1.9 & 1.0 & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{59} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & 0.729 & 1.0 & 1.9 & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{60} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & 0.729 & 1.0 & 1.0 & 10 \\ 
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{\textbf{Description of the parameters used to determine the best acceleration coefficients for NBPSO.} The balanced moderate configuration ($c_p = c_g = 1.49445$) is reused from the winning case of \tableref{Cases_Determine_NBPSO_Pop_Gen} as a fourth comparison point. The two asymmetric cases (58, 59) keep the total acceleration constant ($c_p + c_g = 2.9$) in order to isolate the effect of the cognitive/social repartition, while Case 60 tests a reduced total acceleration. All configurations satisfy the convergence condition \eqref{eq:pso_convergence_condition}: $c_p + c_g \leq 2.9 \leq 2(\lambda+1) = 3.458$.}
    \label{tab:Cases_Determine_NBPSO_C1_C2}
\end{table}

\paragraph{Inertia} Denoting the best acceleration coefficients as $c_p^*$ and $c_g^*$, we finally investigate the inertia weight $\lambda$ (\tableref{Cases_Determine_NBPSO_Inertia}). The inertia weight controls the momentum of the particles. A high value facilitates global exploration, while a low value facilitates local exploitation. 

\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
        \hline 
        \textbf{Case n°} & \textbf{Swarm Size ($S$)} & \textbf{Iterations ($G$)} & $\lambda$ & $c_p$ & $c_g$ & \textbf{Run per algo} \\ \hline

        \textbf{61} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & 0.9 & $c_p^*$ & $c_g^*$ & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{62} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & 0.4 & $c_p^*$ & $c_g^*$ & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{63} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & 0.729 & $c_p^*$ & $c_g^*$ & 10 \\ 
        \hline
    \end{tabular}
    \caption{\textbf{Description of the parameters used to determine the best inertia weight strategy for NBPSO.} The static value $\lambda = 0.729$ is reused from the winning case of \tableref{Cases_Determine_NBPSO_C1_C2} as a fourth comparison point. By construction of the previous stage, $c_p^* + c_g^* \leq 2.989$, so the convergence condition \eqref{eq:pso_convergence_condition} requires $\lambda \geq 0.494$: it is satisfied by all tested values, including the lower bound of the LDIW schedule, which is set to $0.5$ instead of the classical $0.4$ for this reason.}
    \label{tab:Cases_Determine_NBPSO_Inertia}
\end{table}

\subsubsection{Upgraded Particle Swarm Optimization}

Alongside static values ($0.9$ and $0.4$), we also implement and test a Linearly Decreasing Inertia Weight (LDIW), which starts at $0.9$ and linearly decreases to $0.4$ over the iterations to dynamically shift the search from \emph{exploration} to \emph{exploitation}.

\begin{equation}\label{eq:ldiw}
    \lambda(t) = \lambda_{\max} - \left(\lambda_{\max} - \lambda_{\min}\right)\frac{t}{G}, 
    \qquad \lambda_{\max} = 0.9, \quad \lambda_{\min} = 0.5.
\end{equation}

        \textbf{64} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & LDIW(0.9;0.5) & $c_p^*$ & $c_g^*$ & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{65} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & LDIW(0.9;0.4) & $c_p^*$ & $c_g^*$ & 10 \\ \hdashline
        \textbf{66} & $S^*$ & $G^*$ & LDIW(0.9;0.2) & $c_p^*$ & $c_g^*$ & 10 \\ 
        \hline
    \end{tabular}

\end{table}

\subsection{Running experiment}\label{sec:running_experiment}

We will start by finding an optimal set of parameters for the differential evolution \ref{sec:differential_evolution_parameters}, then for the simulated annealing \ref{sec:simulated_annealing_parameters} and finally for the Particle Swarm Optimization \ref{sec:NBPSO_parameters}.

For every table of results corresponding to a specific dataset the color green is used to highlight the best score and the best average score for each dataset and the color red for the worst ; \textit{Method} [10x] indicates ten iterations of the given \textit{method} and best* (best* indicates the best value from each 10 runs), worst, average and standard deviation (std), computation time, Number of Function Evaluations (NFE) and number of average features are provided for each case.

\subsubsection{Differential evolution}\label{sec:differential_evolution_parameters}

The section \ref{sec:BDE_parameters} presents the numerical results for Binary Differential Evolution and section \ref{sec:AMDE_parameters} presents them for Angle Modulation Differential Evolution.

\subsubsubsection{Binary Differential Evolution}\label{sec:BDE_parameters}

\paragraph{Strategy to choose the base vector} First, regarding cases presented in \ref{sec:cases_bde}, we study the impact of the strategy to choose the base vector (cases 1 to 8).

The \tableref{results_BDE_strategy_d174} presents the results for the dataset 174. We can observe that the results using the heavy model are slightly different than using the light model. Furthermore, using the same model, the fitness values are close and most of them are even equal (see for instance the worst best case for case 1, 3, 4, 5 and 6). Even the average scores over 10 runs are very close (and even equal for cases 1 and 2 for the heavy model but in that case, we can see that the standard deviation of case 1 is slightly smaller than for the case 2).

As it was expected, we get significantly generally better score using the heavy model than the light model. Taking into account the average best score, it seems that the case 1 is the better for this dataset.

Base on our previous analysis and observation, it is hard to see a strategy that is broadly emerging. That is the reason why we use a statistical test to distinguish the small difference between all our configurations (see \ref{sec:friedman}).

\tableref{detailed_friedman_ranks} reports the rank obtained by each of the six mutation strategies within every (dataset, model) block, where rank~$1$ denotes the best (highest) mean accuracy and tied accuracies share the average of the ranks they span. 

First, we can see that no strategy is uniformly dominant across all eight blocks, which confirms that the differences are too subtle to be settled by visual inspection.

Then, \textit{best/1} (Case~2) is nonetheless the most consistent performer: it attains the lowest average rank not only globally ($1.94$) but also within each model taken separately (SVM: $1.75$ and kNN: $2.12$). It is, in fact, the only strategy that ranks first under both evaluators, whereas its closest competitor \textit{rand/2} (global rank $2.56$) and the remaining schemes never lead on more than one model. It is however worth observing that \textit{best/1} and \textit{rand/2} have two opposite objectives, respectively fast \emph{exploitation} and long \emph{exploration}. The two \textit{current-to-*} variants ($4.19$ each) and especially \textit{best/2} ($4.75$) clearly fail to solve the problem.

        \hline
        \multicolumn{2}{|c|}{\multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Evaluation Block}}} & \multicolumn{6}{c|}{\textbf{Ranks per Mutation Strategy (Case)}} \\ \cline{3-8}
        \multicolumn{2}{|c|}{} & \textbf{1 (rand/1)} & \textbf{2 (best/1)} & \textbf{3 (c-t-r/1)} & \textbf{4 (c-t-b/1)} & \textbf{5 (rand/2)} & \textbf{6 (best/2)} \\ \hline
        
        \multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\textbf{SVM}}} 
        & 174 &  \cellcolor{best}1.5 & \cellcolor{best}1.5 & 6.0 & 4.5 & 3.0 & 4.5 \\ 
        & 17  & 4.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 5.0 & 3.0 & 2.0 & 6.0 \\ 
        & 52  & 5.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 2.5 & 6.0 & 2.5 & 4.0 \\ 
        & 151 & 3.5 & 3.5 & 5.0 & 6.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 2.0 \\ 

        \hdashline
        
        \multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\textbf{$k-$NN}}} 
        & 174 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 3.0 & 2.0 & 6.0 & 4.0 & 5.0 \\
        & 17  & 3.0 & 2.0 & 4.0 & 5.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 6.0 \\
        & 52  & 6.0 & 2.5 & 4.5 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 2.5 & 4.5 \\
        & 151 & 3.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 4.5 & 2.0 & 4.5 & 6.0 \\
        
        \hline
        
        \multirow{3}{*}{\textbf{Avg. Rank}} 
        & \textbf{SVM only} & 3.50 & \cellcolor{best}\textbf{1.75} & 4.62 & 4.88 & 2.12 & 4.12 \\
        & \textbf{$k-$NN only} & 3.25 & \cellcolor{best}\textbf{2.12} & 3.75 & 3.50 & 3.00 & 5.38 \\ \cline{2-8} 
        & \textbf{GLOBAL}   & 3.38 & \cellcolor{best}\textbf{1.94} & 4.19 & 4.19 & 2.56 & 4.75 \\ \hline
    \end{tabular}
    }
    \caption{\centering\textbf{Detailed Friedman ranks across the 8 evaluation blocks.} Rank 1 is the best (highest accuracy); tied accuracies share the average of their spanned ranks. The final rows display the pooled average ranks.}
    \label{tab:detailed_friedman_ranks}
\end{table}

\tableref{statistical_tests_bde} summarizes the Friedman test applied to these ranks. Runned over the $N=8$ blocks, the test rejects the null hypothesis that all strategies are equivalent ($\chi_F^2 = 13.70$, $p = 0.0176$), a conclusion also common with the less conservative Iman--Davenport statistic ($F_F = 3.65$, $p = 0.0093$).

The distinction between the two statistics is indeed significant: on the SVM blocks alone, the $\chi^2$ approximation fails to reach significance ($p = 0.075$) whereas the Iman--Davenport variant does ($p = 0.045$). This illustrates the fact discussed in \ref{sec:friedman} that the $\chi^2$ approximation is more effective when the number of blocks is small. On the kNN blocks neither statistic is significant ($p = 0.24$), so the discriminating power of the analysis comes essentially from the pooled comparison. The Nemenyi post-hoc test, finally, is necessarily strict with so few blocks: globally it separates only \textit{best/1} from \textit{best/2} (rank difference $2.81 > \mathrm{CD} = 2.67$), and per model the critical difference ($3.77$ for $N=4$) is too large for any pair to be declared significant.


\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
    \resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \begin{tabular}{|l|c c | c c | c | c|}
        \hline
        \multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Scope}} & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textbf{Friedman Test}} & \multicolumn{2}{c|}{\textbf{Iman--Davenport}} & \textbf{Nemenyi} & \multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Separable Pairs}} \\ \cline{2-5}
        & $\chi_F^2$ ($df=5$) & $p$-value & $F_F$ & $p$-value & \textbf{CD} ($\alpha=0.05$) & \\ \hline
        \textbf{Global} ($N=8$)   & 13.70 & \textbf{0.0176} & 3.646 ($df=5, 35$) & \textbf{0.0093} & 2.666 & Case 2 $>$ Case 6 ($d=2.81$) \\ \hline
        \textbf{SVM only} ($N=4$) & 10.00 & 0.0752          & 3.000 ($df=5, 15$) & \textbf{0.0450} & 3.770 & None \\ \hline
        \textbf{$k-$NN only} ($N=4$) & 6.75  & 0.2398          & 1.529 ($df=5, 15$) & 0.2398          & 3.770 & None \\ \hline
    \end{tabular}
    }
    \caption{\centering\textbf{Summary of statistical significance tests for mutation strategies.} Statistically significant $p$-values ($<0.05$) are highlighted in bold. The Critical Difference (CD) dictates the threshold for Nemenyi pairwise significance.}
    \label{tab:statistical_tests_bde}
\end{table}

On the basis of our above results, we adopt \textit{best/1} as $Strat^*$. The choice is the made by three different and converging arguments: the global Friedman test establishes that the strategies differ; \textit{best/1} obtains the best average rank both globally and under each evaluator, making it robust to the choice of the fitness model; and the Nemenyi test confirms that it significantly outperforms the weakest scheme, \textit{best/2}.

This outcome is coherent with the nature of the datasets used in this study, which contain a comparatively small number of features. Because the binary search space grows as $2^d$ with the number of features $d$, a low-dimensional problem yields a search space small enough for the risk of premature convergence to a poor local optimum to remain limited. In that kind of problem, \emph{exploitation} can be encourage over \emph{exploration}. As mentionned in \ref{sec:DE_theory_principle}, a \textit{best}-based strategy encourages more \emph{exploitation} rather than a \textit{rand}-base strategy that foster \emph{exploration}.

As \textit{best/2} add a second vector, there is more perturbation and that is the reason why this is the worst strategy even if it uses the same base strategy as the best strategy \textit{best/1}.

Once the base vector already exploits the best solution, additional perturbation only delays convergence without uncovering better optima, and the more exploratory \textit{rand}-based and \textit{current-to-*} schemes bring no measurable benefit on these problems.

We therefore retain $\boxed{Strat^*=\textit{best/1}}$ for the subsequent tuning of the ration $NP$ and $G$, and later for $F$ and $CR$.


\paragraph{Population/generation ratio} We are now able to run and analyze cases 7, 8 and 9 to determine the best population/generation ratio.

In this study, we added the case called "8bis" which is the case 8 with his parameter reversed (so $NP=200$ and $G=49$).

\tableref{results_BDE_popgen_d174} reports data for dataset 174. The results on this dataset highlight the difference in behavior between the two evaluation models. Using the heavy SVM model, Case 8 ($NP=50$ and $G=199$) is the distinct winner, achieving both the highest peak accuracy $(97.4\%)$ and the best average score $(93.33\%)$. Conversely, the light $k-$NN model struggles with this configuration and performs better with Case 7 ($NP=100$ and $G=99$), suggesting that the $k-$NN evaluator relies more heavily on the initial diversity of a larger population to avoid local optima on this specific dataset.

However, the $k-$NN model strongly favors Case 8bis ($NP=200$ and $G=49$), achieving its best average $(93.86\%)$ and peak score $(95.6\%)$. This extreme configuration shows that the $k-$NN model promotes huge initial exploration of the search space, even if it means iterating fewer times (only $49$ times).

\tableref{statistical_tests_popgen_bde} presents the computation both for $\chi$ and $p-$values and it shows that only the heavy SVM model produces significant distance between case $7$ and $8$. Therefore, the only statistical better case is the case 8, which is totally consistent with our previous observations on all datasets.

With a budget of $\sim$ 10,000 evaluations, investing in more generations (refinement) rather than a large population pays off, but reducing the population to 20 (Case 9) starts to come at the expense of diversity (and therefore of \emph{exploration}). This also confirms that case 8bis (with the inversion of $NP$ and $G$, hence with fewer generations) is the weakest and that the swap was indeed suboptimal.

To put it in a nutshell, we are now able to set the best ratio overall with the configuration of the Case 8 so we have:

\[
\boxed{
    \begin{cases}
        NP^*&=50\\
        G^*&=199
    \end{cases}
}
\]

\paragraph{Scaling factor and crossover probability} Once we have $NP^*$ and $G^*$, we are able to start our grid search for both scaling factor $F$ and crossover probability $CR$.

The results on dataset 174 (\tableref{results_BDE_FCR_d174}) show a preference for moderate to high perturbation, with the crossover probability. The heavy SVM model has its best average score $(93.85\%)$ with case 16 (width $F=0.5$ and $CR=0.7$) and case 19 (with $F=0.9$ and $CR=0.5$). The light $k-$NN model tends to need even higher perturbation as he has the best average score of $87.44\%$ with both cases 20 and 21 (with $F=0.9$ and $CR \geq 0.7$). On this specific dataset, having a lot of perturbation with high scaling factor and crossover probability helps to avoid being stuck in a local optima and provide both \emph{exploration} and \emph{exploitation}.

On dataset 17 (\tableref{results_BDE_FCR_d17}), the optimal parameters are more scattered. The SVM model has its highest average score of $97.19\%$ cases 12 (with $F=0.3$ and $CR=0.7$) and 14 (with $F=0.5$ and $CR=0.1$) that are very different in behavior. On the other hand, the $k-$NN model needs the balanced case 19 (with $F=0.9$ and $CR=0.5$) to achieve its best average score of $94.91\%$. This shows a trade-off between both models: the SVM prefers a smaller scaling factor combined with higher crossover, whereas the $k-$NN model require bigger scaling factor and therefore smaller crossover probability to maintain convergence.

Faced with the high dimensionality of 60 features, the algorithms tend to converge toward balanced configurations to maintain enough \emph{exploration} (\tableref{results_BDE_FCR_d151}). The SVM model achieves its best average score $(85.95\%)$ with cases 15 (with $F=0.5$ and $CR=0.5$) and 16 (with $CR=0.7$). The $k-$NN model needs the same type of configuration as it reaches the best average score with cases 13 (width $F=0.3$ and $CR=0.9$) and 15 $(80.48\%)$. As we are handling bigger and complex search space, extreme values (like $F=0.9$ or $CR=0.1$) are generally penalized, and the standard balanced parameters are able to get a better trad-off to reach an equilibrium between fast convergence and avoid local optima.

To sum up, we will fix this final set of parameters for the tuning of Binary Differential Evolution:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{cases}
    F &= 0.3 \\
    CR &= 0.7
\end{cases}
}.
$$

The tuning of the Binary Differential Evolution is now finished and we will now use the same protocol to set up the Angle Modulation Differential Evolution.

\subsubsubsection{Angle Modulation Differential Evolution}\label{sec:AMDE_parameters}

\paragraph{Strategy to choose the base vector} First, as the mutation strategy has a huge impact on the very goal or our algorithm and on all others parameters, we start by studying the score of different mutation strategies (cases 22 to 27 from \tableref{Cases_Determine_AMDE_Strategy}).

The results on dataset 174 (\tableref{results_AMDE_strategy_d174}) show that the AMDE algorithm behaves differently from the Binary Differential Evolution previously set up. For the heavy SVM model, Case 22 (\textit{rand/1}) achieves the highest average score $(93.85\%)$, outperforming the \textit{best}-based strategies. The light $k-$NN model, however, finds its best average performance with Case 24 (\textit{current-to-rand/1}) at $86.92\%$. As AMDE optimizes a 4-dimensional space instead of a discrete sequence, both models seem to benefit significantly from the exploratory nature of \textit{rand}-based configuration to avoid getting trapped in local optima, especially on "hard" dataset. However, depending on the dataset difficulty, some \textit{best}-based strategies performs good as well.

We can observe, as it was the case with BDE, that all scores are very close. All best scores for the heavy model are very close and, in general, the standard deviation is very small regarding the average score.

Dataset 17 (\tableref{results_AMDE_strategy_d17}) reinforces the preference for the \textit{rand/1} strategy under the SVM evaluator (corresponding to case 22 with the best average score $(97.37\%)$ and has also the best score $(98.2\%)$. For the $k-$NN model, the results are even more tight, with Case 23 (\textit{best/1}) and Case 24 (\textit{current-to-rand/1}) having the highest average accuracy $(94.56\%)$.

Despite this little difference with the light model, the strong and consistent superiority of Case 22 on the heavy model suggests that exploration remains the main objective for this strategy, meaning that this might a complex dataset.

Dataset 52 (\tableref{results_AMDE_strategy_d52}) introduces a slight shift in behavior. For the SVM model, the strategies utilizing two difference vectors (with case 26 (\textit{rand/2}) and case 27 (\textit{best/2})) share the best average score $(96.48\%)$. This means that adding perturbation helps the algorithm so it might indicates that we need a larger \emph{exploration} phase on this specific topography. On the other hand, the $k-$NN model strongly favors case 24 (\textit{current-to-rand/1}) with an average of $93.10\%$ (and also the best score).

On this dataset specifically, we can highlight a major difference between the light and heavy models: the $k-$NN model selects drastically fewer features (around 5 to 6) compared to the SVM (around 17 to 18), regardless of the mutation strategy employed. This might be due to the complexity of our evaluation.

On dataset 151 (\tableref{results_AMDE_strategy_d151}), which contains the highest number of features, the margins between strategies are extremely narrow for the SVM model. Cases 22, 25, and 26 have all the best average score $(84.29\%)$, with identical peak scores $(88.1\%)$. For the $k-$NN model, case 26 (\textit{rand/2}) emerges as the distinct winner with an average of $81.19\%$ even if its not reaching the best score.

We can also see that the case 27 (\textit{best/2}) has the poorest average accuracy for both models, indicating that combining strong exploitation (the \textit{best} base vector) with heavy perturbation (two difference vectors) is counterproductive in this specific configuration and dataset.

However, because the number of cases and datasets are small, the $p-$value does not allow us to determine significative difference among the cases (\tableref{statistical_tests_AMDE_strategy}). That is the reason why we will take into account the global average rank from the Friedman test, which leads us to conclude that the case 23 is the best overall.

\begin{table}[H]

    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
    \resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \begin{tabular}{|l|c c | c c | c | c|}

        & $\chi_F^2$ ($df=5$) & $p$-value & $F_F$ & $p$-value & \textbf{CD} ($\alpha=0.05$) & \\ \hline
        \textbf{Global} (${N=8}$) & 3.333 & 0.6487 & 0.636 (${df=5, 35}$) & 0.6734 & 2.666 & None \\ \hline

    \end{tabular}
    }
    \caption{\textbf{Summary of statistical significance tests for the AMDE mutation strategies.} The Critical Difference (CD) sets the threshold for Nemenyi pairwise significance. No significant differences were detected ($p \ge 0.05$); the best strategy ($Strat^{**}=$ best/1, Case~23) is therefore selected on the lowest global average rank.}
    \label{tab:statistical_tests_AMDE_strategy}
\end{table}

To sum up, we will use the same strategy than for the standard Binary Differential Evolution with the \textit{best} strategy but with small perturbation (only one vector) allowing \emph{exploitation} but avoiding premature convergence: $\boxed{Strat^{**} = best/1}$.

\paragraph{Population/generation ratio} Now that we have set up the mutation strategy, we can study the impact of different population/generation ratio, considering a smaller population as we are handling 4-dimensional problem for AMDE.

The case "28bis" is the same case as case 28, but in reverse, so case "28bis" corresponds to $NP=200$ and $G=49$ so a huge population but fewer generations.

The results on dataset 174 (\tableref{results_AMDE_popgen_d174}) reveal a stark contrast in the requirements of the two evaluation models when optimizing the 4-dimensional AMDE space. The heavy SVM model performs best with case 28bis, having the highest average accuracy $(93.08\%)$ and penalizing the case 30 with a very small population (only 10 individuals). On the contrary, the light $k-$NN model produces opposite results: it achieves its highest average $(86.92\%)$ and best score with case 30. This suggests that for this specific dataset, the $k-$NN evaluator requires precise and long tuning of the trigonometric parameters over many generations (more than 1000) rather than a broad initial sampling, corresponding to the total number of individuals.

On dataset 17 (\tableref{results_AMDE_popgen_d17}), the SVM model shifts away from extreme diversity and finds its optimal balance with case 28 (with $NP=50$ and $G=199$), having the best average score of $96.93\%$. The $k-$NN model, however, continues to favor deeper evolutionary searches with many generations and a small population size, with case 29 (with $NP=20$ and $G=499$) having the best average performance of $95.09\%$.

We can also observe that for the $k-$NN model, the high-population configuration (case 28bis) performs the poorest, confirming that spreading the computational budget across too many individuals prevents the algorithm from sufficiently refining the 4 continuous parameters with enough generations.

On this dataset also, every configuration achieve to reach the same best score.

        & \textbf{std}   & 0.95 & 1.44 & 1.37 & 1.37 & 1.23 & 1.90 & 0.45 & 1.71 \\ \hline

        \textbf{NFE} & \textbf{number} & 10000 & 10000 & 10000 & 10010 & 10000 & 10000 & 10000 & 10010 \\ \hline

    \end{tabular}

    \caption{\textbf{AMDE results on dataset~17 for the population/generation ratio selection (cases 28, 28bis, 29 and 30).}}
    \label{tab:results_AMDE_popgen_d17}
\end{table}

Dataset 52 (\tableref{results_AMDE_popgen_d52}) produces the same general results as observed on Dataset 17, leading us to asses that both datasets share common characteristics. The SVM model remains highly stable and performs best with the balanced case 28, which produces both the highest average $(96.34\%)$ and the best peak score $(98.6\%)$. The $k-$NN model achieves its best average accuracy $(92.54\%)$ with case 30 with a very small population but many generations (with $NP=10$ and $G=999$) so a huge \emph{exploitation}.

As it was previously the case on dataset 52 (\tableref{results_AMDE_strategy_d52}), there is still a significant difference in the number of selected features: the $k-$NN selects much fewer features (5 or 6 features over its 1000 generations), while the SVM retains around 17 to 18 features.

Dataset 151, which possesses the highest number of features with 60 features, forces a convergence in behavior between the two models. Both the SVM and the $k-$NN penalize the high-population and low-generation setup case 28bis, which has the worst average scores for both.

On this dataset, the SVM reaches the best average score with case 30 $(85.24\%)$, which emphasize \emph{exploitation} with 1000 generations on a small population of 10 individuals. On the other hand, the $k-$NN model shares the best average $(79.76\%)$ with case 29 and case 30, both also fosterinf \emph{exploitation} with many generations over a relatively small generation.

Because mapping the trigonometric generating function to a 60-bit mask (corresponding to the 60 features) requires extreme precision in the selection of the four $a,b,c,d$ parameters, both evaluators require high number of generations (499 to 999) to reach good score. This dataset is indeed the most difficult as it is proven by all scores in this table.

Having all these results, we are now able to compute ranks for every cases using Friedman test (\tableref{detailed_friedman_ranks_AMDE_popgen}). We can see that the case 28 is the best when we take into account only the SVM model and both models whereas case 30 (that foster massively \emph{exploitation} with 999 generations over 10 individuals) is the best using only the light model.

However, computing the $p-$value, there is no significant statistical difference between all these cases (\tableref{statistical_tests_AMDE_popgen}). Therefore, we choose the best global case from the test, which is furthermore also the best case for heavy model, so we set up case 28.

            & $\chi_F^2$ ($df=3$) & $p$-value & $F_F$ & $p$-value & \textbf{CD} ($\alpha=0.05$) & \\ \hline
            \textbf{Global} (${N=8}$) & 2.487 & 0.4777 & 0.809 (${df=3, 21}$) & 0.5030 & 1.658 & None \\ \hline

    \caption{\textbf{Summary of statistical significance tests for the AMDE population/generation ratios.} The Critical Difference (CD) sets the threshold for Nemenyi pairwise significance. No significant differences were detected ($p \ge 0.05$); the best parameter ($NP=50/G=199$, Case~28) is therefore selected based on the lowest global average rank and its dominance on the SVM evaluator.}
    \label{tab:statistical_tests_AMDE_popgen}
\end{table}

To sum up, we choose the balanced configuration that makes possible both \emph{exploration} (with a sufficient amount of individuals) and \emph{exploitation} (with enough generation to exploit a promising region of the search space). We therefore set the following parameters from case 28:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{cases}
    NP^{**} &= 50\\
    G^{**} &= 199
\end{cases}
}
$$

\paragraph{Scaling factor and crossover probability} Having the exact population size and the number of generations, we are now able to run the grid search to set the scaling factor and the crossover probability.

The results on dataset 174 (\tableref{results_AMDE_FCR_d174}) is totally coherent with our first intuition on the principle of AMDE and its use of only parameters (see \ref{sec:cases_amde}): it should foster high crossover probability because, otherwise, it would break the inherent coherence of the generated wave. Both models strongly favor indeed high crossover probabilities. The heavy SVM model achieves its best average score $(92.31\%)$ with case 33 and a crossover probability of $0.9$, while the light $k-$NN model has its best average score $(87.18\%$) with case 37 and a crossover probability also equal to $0.9$. 

On the contrary, configurations with low crossover rates ($CR=0.1$ for instance), such as cases 31, 35, and 39, have always the poorest average accuracies, confirming that updating the $a,b,c,d$ parameters simultaneously is crucial to maintain a coherent trigonometric mapping on this dataset.

Dataset 17 presents data that does not confirm that observation on the crossover probability for the 4-dimensional search space (\tableref{results_AMDE_FCR_d17}). Both evaluators achieve their best average scores using a very low crossover probability of $CR=0.1$. The SVM model has its best average score $(97.37\%)$ with case 39 (with $F=0.9$ and $CR=0.1$), while, on the other hand, the $k-$NN model performs best with case 35 (with $F=0.5$ and $CR=0.1$) and an average score of $94.74\%$. This shows that, for this specific dataset, fine-tuning the trigonometric wave by mutating only one or two continuous parameters at a time (low crossover probability) is more effective than fully overwriting the vector (with a high crossover probability), provided the scaling factor $F$ provides enough momentum or inertia.

Again, we can also observe that almost configurations manage to reach the best score, especially with the light model. Furthermore, the light model has generally less good score than with the heavy model.

On dataset 52 (\tableref{results_AMDE_FCR_d52}), our initial statement on the crossover probability is more observed as the models have preference for high crossover rates. Case 34 (with $F=0.3$ and $CR=1.0$ and denoted in yellow in the table) manage to have the best average score for both light $(94.51\%)$ and heavy model $(96.48\%)$ with a significant low standard deviation of $0.80$ and $1.37$ respectively. A crossover rate of 1.0 means the trial vector is entirely composed of mutated values (ignoring the parent vector completely), demonstrating that huge perturbation combined with a limited scaling factor (such as $F=0.3$) is highly efficient on this dataset.

With the high dimensionality of dataset 151 (60 original features), the behavior of the two models diverges significantly (\tableref{results_AMDE_FCR_d151}). The SVM model benefits from extreme \emph{exploration}: case 42 (with $F=0.9$ and $CR=1.0$) and case 31 (with $F=0.3$ and $CR=0.1$) have the best average $(84.29\%)$ but only case 42 manage to achieve the best score of $95.2\%$, showing that maximum disruption with high crossover and high scaling factor can uncover excellent feature subsets. The $k-$NN model, on the other hand, struggles with such perturbation and prefers a much more conservative approach, achieving its best average $(80.00\%)$ with case 35 (with $F=0.5$ and $CR=0.1$). These different behaviors highlight how differently the two models handle the mapping of the 4 parameters into this big 60-bit binary mask.

Therefore, we will keep for the final set of parameters the configuration from case 42:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{cases}
    F^{**} &= 0,9\\
    CR^{**} &= 1,0    
\end{cases}
}
$$

\subsubsection{Simulated Annealing}\label{sec:simulated_annealing_parameters}

\paragraph{Parameters} We will now present the single tuning of the two parameters of the Simulated Annealing: the cooling rate and the initial temperature. As they are exponentially dependent (see \ref{sec:simulated_annealing_theoretical_reviews}), we present now the results from the grid search proposed in \ref{sec:cases_sa}.

The results on dataset 174 (\tableref{results_SA_schedule_d174}) reveal a clear distinction between the two evaluation models regarding the required physical energy of the system.

The heavy SVM model achieves its best average score $(88.97\%)$ with case 49 (with $T_0=0.1$ and $\alpha=0.999)$, showing hence a preference for a moderate initial temperature followed by rapid cooling ($T/T_0 \approx 4.5\cdot10^{-5}$). On the contrary, the light $k-$NN model performs best with the highly exploratory cases 53 and 54 (with higher temperature $T_0=1$), showing that it requires maximum initial kinetic energy and slower cooling to effectively avoid local optima.

In general, both models strongly penalize the lowest temperature settings with $T_0=0.001$ and $T_0=0.01$, which tells us that \emph{exploration} phase is really necessary.

On dataset 17 (\tableref{results_SA_schedule_d17}), we can observe the same behavior as observed on dataset 174. The SVM model once again is more accurate using case 49 (with $T_0=0.1$ and $\alpha=0.999$), reaching an average accuracy of $97.37\%$ and a best score of $99.1\%$. On the other hand, the $k-$NN model favors case 54 in average even if its not able to reach the best score (with $T_0=1$ and $\alpha=0.9999$), which represents the slowest cooling schedule tested ($T/T_0 \approx 0.37$). This opposition confirms that while the SVM landscape allows for quick convergence into an optimal feature subset, the $k-$NN landscape contains more local optima and requires the algorithm to remain in \emph{exploration} longer.

On dataset 52 (\tableref{results_SA_schedule_d52}), both models obtain bad score with low initial temperature ($T_0=0.001$), with cases 43 and 44 having the poorest averages. The SVM model shifts slightly from the behavior observed in the last dataset, finding its best average $(95.07\%)$ with case 51 (with $T_0=0.1$ and $\alpha=0.9999$) instead of case 49 on the previous dataset. We can therefore assess that this dataset requires the SVM to maintain \emph{exploration} slightly longer as the cooling rate is here lower. The $k-$NN model maintains its preference for slow cooling ($\alpha=0.9999$ or $0.9995$), securing its best average $(89.44\%)$ with cases 48 and 50, that confirm its need to have enough \emph{exploration}.

Dataset 151 (\tableref{results_SA_schedule_d151}) contains again the highest number of original features (60), which will have impact on the cooling rate.

Results on both models show that rapid cooling (such as $\alpha=0.999$) failed to solve the problem as it suffers of premature convergence. The SVM model achieves its best average $(84.52\%)$ with case 48 (with $T_0=0.01$ and $\alpha=0.9999$), while the $k-$NN model has its best average result $(83.57\%$ with case 51 (with $T_0=0.1$ and $\alpha=0.9999$). In this high-dimensional context, both classifiers require the algorithm to utilize the slowest cooling rate $(\alpha=0.9999)$ to ensure enough \emph{exploration} and no premature convergence.
