To sump up, the final overall best parameters for the Simulated Annealing are as follows:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{cases}
    T_0^* &= 0.1\\
    \alpha^* & =0.9995
\end{cases}
},
$$

which are a nice balance between enough \emph{exploration} (not too high cooling rate) and enough \emph{exploration} (enough energy with the initial temperature).

\subsubsection{Novel Binary Particle Swarm Optimization}\label{sec:NBPSO_parameters}

We will now present the tuning for the Novel Binary Particle Swarm Optimization and we start with the search for the best ratio swarm size/iterations.

\paragraph{Swarm size/iterations ratio} The results on dataset 174 (\tableref{results_NBPSO_SG_d174}) show a clear opposition between the heavy and light models regarding swarm dynamics. The SVM model achieves its best average score $(94.10\%)$ with the balanced case 55 (with $S=100$ and $G=99$), showing the need for a larger initial swarm $S$ to have enough \emph{exploration}. On the contrary, the $k-$NN model performs best with smaller swarm size with case 57 (with $S=20$ and $G=499$) at $87.18\%$ so it needs more iterations to behave in an \emph{exploitation} phase.

On dataset 17 (\tableref{results_NBPSO_SG_d17}), the difference between scores is even smaller but we can notice a slightly different behavior. The SVM model achieves its highest average $(97.19\%)$ with case 57, though case 55 is the second $(97.02\%)$ with a slightly smaller standard deviation. using this model on this dataset, the need for a huge \emph{exploration} phase is less important than having enough \emph{exploitation}. The $k-$NN model finds its optimal balance with the intermediate case 56 (with $S=50$ and $G=199$) at $94.47\%$, using an equilibrium between \emph{exploration} and \emph{exploitation}.

Dataset 52 (\tableref{results_NBPSO_SG_d52}) brings the SVM model back to the previous best configuration with bigger swarm size. Case 55 has the best average accuracy $(95.92\%)$ and has also the best score $(98.6\%)$. The $k-$NN model once again prefers \emph{exploitation} phase with more iterations, achieving its best average score $(90.70\%)$ with case 56.

We can also notice that the average number of features selects is almost constant despite these different dynamic preferences within each model.

On the other hand, the $k-$NN model, achieves its best performance $(80.24\%)$ with the highly iterative case 57 (with $S=20$ and $G=499$) that promote more \emph{exploitation} and need more iterations to converge.

Using statistics (\tableref{detailed_friedman_ranks_S_G}), it confirms our observation: the SVM model shows a statistically significant preference $(p\approx0.0388)$ for the high swarm size configuration of case 55, achieving the best average rank of $1.25$. The Nemenyi post-hoc test separates case 55 from case 56 for this model (\tableref{statistical_tests_S_G}).

\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.2}
    %\resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \begin{tabular}{| c | l | c c c |}
        \hline
        \multicolumn{2}{|c|}{\multirow{2}{*}{\textbf{Evaluation Block}}} & \multicolumn{3}{c|}{\textbf{Ranks per Swarm Size / Iterations Combination ($S$/$G$ Case)}} \\ \cline{3-5}
        \multicolumn{2}{|c|}{} & \textbf{55} & \textbf{56} & \textbf{57} \\ \hline
        \multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\textbf{SVM}}}
        & 174 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 3.0 & 2.0 \\
        & 17  & 2.0 & 3.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 \\
        & 52  & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 3.0 & 2.0 \\
        & 151 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 & 3.0 & 2.0 \\
        \hdashline
        \multirow{4}{*}{\rotatebox[origin=c]{90}{\textbf{$k-$NN}}}
        & 174 & 3.0 & 2.0 & \cellcolor{best}1.0 \\

    \label{tab:detailed_friedman_ranks_S_G}
\end{table}

On the other hand, the $k-$NN model shows no statistically significant preference even if the best rank are obtained with cases 56 and 57 with more iterations than case 55.

Because the SVM explicitly requires case 55 for optimal performance and the $k-$NN shows no significant statistical difference for it, we choose the balanced case 55 (with $S=100$ and $G=99$) to continue our tuning.

\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
    \resizebox{\linewidth}{!}{
    \begin{tabular}{|l|c c | c c | c | c|}

        & $\chi_F^2$ ($df=2$) & $p$-value & $F_F$ & $p$-value & \textbf{CD} ($\alpha=0.05$) & \\ \hline
        \textbf{Global} (${N=8}$) & 1.750 & 0.4169 & 0.860 (${df=2, 14}$) & 0.4445 & 1.172 & None \\ \hline
        \textbf{SVM only} (${N=4}$) & 6.500 & 0.0388 & 13.000 (${df=2, 6}$) & 0.0066 & 1.657 & 55--56 \\ \hline

    \end{tabular}
    }
    \caption{\textbf{Summary of statistical significance tests for the Swarm Size / Iterations ratios (Cases 55--57).} The Critical Difference (CD) sets the threshold for Nemenyi pairwise significance. No difference is significant globally or for the $k$-NN model ($p \ge 0.05$); for the SVM model the omnibus test is significant and Nemenyi separates Cases 55 and 56.}
    \label{tab:statistical_tests_S_G}
\end{table}

To sum up, we set up the swarm size and the iterations to preserve a trade-off between \emph{exploration} and \emph{explotation} and we now have the following parameters:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{cases}
    S^* &= 100\\
    G^* &= 99
\end{cases}
}.
$$

\paragraph{Personal and social component} We are now able to study the importance of both personal component $c_p$ and social or group component $c_g$.

Both the heavy SVM and the light k-NN models achieve their highest average scores ($96.84\%$ and $94.56\%$, respectively) using the unbalanced case 59 (with $c_p=1.0$ and $c_g=1.9$). In this dataset (\tableref{results_NBPSO_cpcg_d17}), the social component enable converging fast toward the global best solution so fostering \emph{exploitation}. On the contrary, favoring social component produces the poorest score with the case 58 on both models.

On the contrary, dataset 52 (\tableref{results_NBPSO_cpcg_d52}) produces very different results than observed on dataset 17, showing that bigger social component leads to premature convergence. Both models strictly favor the case 58 (with a huge social component $c_p=1.9$ and $c_g=1.0$), with the SVM achieving an average of $96.34\%$ and the $k-$NN of $92.54\%$. In this scenario, encouraging particles to independently exploit their own best solutions rather than using the trend of the whole group is very effective, showing that we need big \emph{exploration} phase on this dataset. For both models indeed case 59 produces the worst results in the entire set of configurations.

However, based on the general observations and the best global rank of case 60, it is empirically and wised to choose the balanced case to continue our tuning. Hence, the parameters are now:

$$
\boxed{
\begin{cases}
    c_p^* &= 1 \\
    c_g &= 1
\end{cases}
}.$$

\paragraph{Inertia} The inertia parameter is also very important to control the trade-off between \emph{exploration} and \emph{exploitation} as it represents the momentum of the particles: if $\lambda$ is huge, particles will have velocity so it enhance \emph{exploration} ; if $\lambda$ is small, it will foster local \emph{exploitation}.

On dataset 174 (\tableref{results_NBPSO_inertia_d174}), we can observe different good configurations depending on the used model. High inertia of case 61 (with $\lambda=0.9$) produces poor results using the heavy SVM model. But this case has the best average and the best score using the light $k-$NN model. Furthermore, cases 62 (with $\lambda=0.4$) and 63 (with $\lambda=0.729$) share the best average score using the heavy model, whereas case 62 produces the worst average score using the light model.

This indicates the SVM favors a quicker deceleration to be more in a \emph{exmploitation} phase and the $k-$NN model, on the contrary, suffer from premature convergence using this inertia and needs more \emph{exploration}.

Furthermore, both models have their poorest average performance with the high inertia of case 61. It is therefore abundantly clear that \emph{exploration} is vital on this dataset.

    \label{tab:results_NBPSO_inertia_d17}
\end{table}

On dataset 52 (\tableref{results_NBPSO_inertia_d52}), we still observe strong difference between both models. The SVM model strictly favors the balanced standard inertia of case 63 $(\lambda=0.729)$, achieving a best average score of $96.20\%$, and the case 62 with low inertia has the worst results. However, the $k-$NN model behaves exactly the opposite: the best average score is obtained using low inertia (case 62 with $91.97\%$) and the best average score using high inertia (case 61 with $90.56\%$) and, again, the scores are closer using light model than the heavy model. We can still notice that the balanced case 63 produces overall good results for both models.

The dataset 151 has the higher dimensional of all our datasets, using a big inertia will inevitably lead to insufficient \emph{exploration}. Both models have indeed their worst average scores using case 61 (with $\lambda=0.9$). The SVM model reaches its best average score using case 62 and the low inertia $(87.86\%)$, while the $k-$NN model prefers the standard balanced inertia of case 63 $(79.29\%)$.

Moreover, there is again no separable pairs using the Nemenyi differentiation test so we need to use only the global ranking.

    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}

    \begin{tabular}{|l|c c | c c | c | c|}

        & $\chi_F^2$ ($df=2$) & $p$-value & $F_F$ & $p$-value & \textbf{CD} ($\alpha=0.05$) & \\ \hline
        \textbf{Global} (${N=8}$) & 5.172 & 0.0753 & 3.344 (${df=2, 14}$) & 0.0650 & 1.172 & None \\ \hline
        \textbf{SVM only} (${N=4}$) & 3.600 & 0.1653 & 2.455 (${df=2, 6}$) & 0.1664 & 1.657 & None \\ \hline

    \end{tabular}
    }
    \caption{\textbf{Summary of statistical significance tests for the static inertia weight $\lambda$ (Cases 61--63).} The Critical Difference (CD) sets the threshold for Nemenyi pairwise significance. No difference is significant at $\alpha=0.05$ (the global test is borderline, $p \approx 0.07$, with the high-inertia Case~61 ranked weakest).}
    \label{tab:statistical_tests_inertia}
\end{table}

However, both case 62 and 63 are equal but case 63 suggest a better trade-off between \emph{exploration} and \emph{exploitation} as its inertia value is higher than case 62. That is the reason why we will no use

$$
\boxed{
    \lambda^* = 0.72
}.
$$



\paragraph{Upgraded} \tableref{results_NBPSO_ldiw_d17}, \tableref{results_NBPSO_ldiw_d52}, \tableref{results_NBPSO_ldiw_d151} and \tableref{results_NBPSO_ldiw_d174} present our results using this upgrade on NBPSO. However, if we compare these results to the preivous one using fixed value of inertia, we can notice that the results using LDIW are always worst than with fixed value. This might be because we started LDIW with a too high value of $\lambda_{max}$ making impossible to converge faster enough to get good results. As we got $\lambda=0.7429$, we should have probably start with this value and then decreasing this value of $\lambda$. The statistical results are also presented in \tableref{detailed_friedman_ranks_ldiw} and in \tableref{statistical_tests_ldiw}. The two best static models ($\lambda=0.4$ and $\lambda=0.729$) outperform all three LDIW models, and the best LDIW model (case 64) barely matches the worst static model ($\lambda=0.9$).

\paragraph{Novel Binary Particle Swarm Optimization} Finally, the parameters for the Novel Binary Particle Swarm Optimization, both with standard and upgraded approach, are proposed in \tableref{final_parameters_NBPSO}.

\begin{table}[H]
    \centering
    \renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
    \begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|c|c|c|c|}
        \hline

\addcontentsline{toc}{subsection}{Evolutionary Algorithms}
\subsection*{Evolutionary Algorithms}\label{sec:evolutionary_algorithms_theoretical_reviews}

\subsubsection*{Overview}

Based on biological evolution, the evolutionary algorithms reproduce this approach to solve such optimization problem. This approach has several steps such as: reproduction, mutation, recombination, selection and elitism (\figref{EA_scheme}). These steps are indeed the main parameters of the method: what is the size of the population? How many generations should we consider? What kind of mutation are we using? With what probability? How do we model the recombination or the crossover? How do we select individual (the member of the population) and do we apply elitism?

All these parameters have an influence on the ratio between exploration and exploitation as discussed in \ref{sec:optimization_problem_exploration_exploitation} and will now succinctly describe these parameters.

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.7\linewidth]{Zero_Project/illustration/EA_scheme.jpg}
    \caption{\textbf{The general scheme of an evolutionary algorithm as a flow-chart.} All the steps of the EA are represented: we initialize a population (randomly), we select the parents, it might be mutation and recombination to form the child to create the new generation of the population. We loop until we run enough generations to have a good solution. The elitism is not represented in this scheme. (Source: \cite{eiben2015evolutionary})}
    \label{fig:EA_scheme}
\end{figure}

\subsubsection*{Selection}

They are two main selection methods: the \emph{roulette} and the \emph{tournament} that we will now present and analyze.

\newpage

\paragraph{Roulette}

\begin{wrapfigure}{r}{0.4\textwidth}
    \centering
    %\vspace{-15pt}
    \includegraphics[width=0.9\linewidth]{Zero_Project/illustration/roulette_scheme.png}
    \caption{\textbf{Roulette selection scheme.} (Source: \cite{przewozniczek_ga_basics})}
    \label{fig:roulette_scheme}
    \vspace{-20pt}
\end{wrapfigure}


Even if this approach is fair, it is highly dependent on the values of the optimized function (because if we add a huge number to the objective function, we will get almost equal probability so an equiprobable situation but the maximum is still the same) so it makes controlling the convergence hard. Finally, this selection's method imposes that fitness must be positive.

For all these reasons, and as state-of-the-art GAs do not use roulette wheel \cite{przewozniczek_ga_basics}, we will not implement this method but the \emph{tournament} instead.

\paragraph{Tournament}

The \emph{tournament} method combines both randomness and selection in order to avoid the issue of the roulette. We start indeed by choosing $k$ individuals randomly and then we select among these $k$ individuals the best individual, who is named "winner of the tournament".

On the one hand, this method helps to control promote best individuals with high values of $k$, which increase the convergence. On the other hand, it helps to promote diversity with low $k$-value, which give a chance for lower-fitting individuals \cite{przewozniczek_ga_basics}.

Therefore, we will implement this selection method and the number $k$ will be one of our parameters.

\subsubsection*{Mutation}

As we use permutation to model the problem, we consider two mutation type: the \emph{swap} and the \emph{inversion}.

\paragraph{Swap} This operation consists of swapping two random elements of the permutation, like two genes in a chromosome. For example, if we take the permutation $\langle \textcolor{centrale_red}{2},4,6,9,1,\textcolor{centrale_red}{3},8\rangle$, we can choose randomly two elements within the permutation. Let's assume we choose $2$ and $3$, then the mutated sequence become $\langle \textcolor{centrale_red}{3},4,6,9,1,\textcolor{centrale_red}{2},8\rangle$.


\paragraph{Inversion} We will implement another mutation method, the \emph{inversion}. In these mutations, we also select two random positions in the sequence but instead of swapping, we revers all elements in between these two positions. Let's take again the example of the sequence $\langle \textcolor{centrale_red}{2},4,6,9,1,\textcolor{centrale_red}{3},8\rangle$ and let's also assume that we choose randomly $2$ and $3$. Then, the mutated sequence become $\langle \textcolor{centrale_red}{3,1,9,6,4,2},8\rangle$. 

\subsubsection*{Crossover}

The \emph{crossover} (or the \emph{recombination} on \figref{EA_scheme}) aims to create the descendant, or the offspring, or the children of the two parents. We will present and compare three crossover : the \emph{Ordered Crossover} (OX), the \emph{Partial Matched Crossover} (PMX) and the \emph{Cycle Crossover} (CX).

\paragraph{Ordered Crossover} In order to run the \emph{ordered crossover}, we start by randomly pick two positions on one parent's sequence. Then, we copy the subsequence between these two positions to the offspring. All others positions are then copy from the second parent (in the order of the parent's sequence after removing the previous subsequence's position).

Let's take the example of parent $P_1$ with the sequence $\langle 2,5,3,4,8,9,1,7,6\rangle$ and parent $P_2$ with the sequence $\langle 1,9,6,2,3,4,5,7,8\rangle$. Let's assume that we pick $5$ and $1$ number from parent $P_1$. Then the ordered crossover operation, noted $\mathcal{R}_{OX}$, can be represented as follows:

\begin{equation}
    \langle 2,\underbrace{\textcolor{centrale_red}{5},3,4,8,9,\textcolor{centrale_red}{1}}_{\substack{\text{we copy this}\\\text{subsequence}}},7,6\rangle \;\; \mathcal{R}_{OX} \;\; \langle \cancel{1},\cancel{9},6,2,\cancel{3},\cancel{4},\cancel{5},7,\cancel{8}\rangle = \langle 6, \underbrace{\textcolor{centrale_red}{5,3,4,8,9,1,}}_{\substack{\text{copied}\\\text{subsequence}}} 2, 7\rangle
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Partial matched crossover} The \emph{partial matched crossover} is an extension of the ordered crossover. We also starts by choosing randomly two elements for one parent in order to get a subsequence. Then, we map the numbers of the corresponding position in the other parent. Finally, the mapping subsequence is copied to the offspring sequence and completed with the remaining mapped sequence of the other parent.

Let's take the example of parent $P_1$ with the sequence $\langle 7,5,3,1,9,8,6,4,2\rangle$ and parent $P_2$ with the sequence $\langle 1,3,5,2,4,6,8,9,7\rangle$. Let's assume that we pick the subsequence between positions 4 and 6 (so between $1$ and $8$) from parent $P_1$. This selection establishes the mapping $1 \longleftrightarrow 2$, $9 \longleftrightarrow 4$, and $8 \longleftrightarrow 6$ with the corresponding elements in $P_2$ as represented as follows:

\begin{align*}
    &\langle 7,5,3,\textcolor{centrale_red}{1,9,8,}6,4,2\rangle\\
    \text{Mapping operation } \longrightarrow& \hspace{1.3cm} \Big\updownarrow  \, \Big\updownarrow \,  \Big\updownarrow\\
    &\langle 1,3,5,\textcolor{centrale_red}{2,4,6,}8,9,7\rangle
\end{align*}
Then the partially mapped crossover operation, noted $\mathcal{R}_{PMX}$, can be represented as follows:

\begin{equation}
    \langle 7,5,3,\underbrace{\textcolor{centrale_red}{1,9,8}}_{\substack{\text{we copy this}\\\text{subsequence}}},6,4,2\rangle \;\; \mathcal{R}_{PMX} \;\; \langle \overset{2}{\cancel{1}},3,5,\underbrace{2,4,6}_{\substack{\text{mapping}\\\text{section}}},\overset{6}{\cancel{8}},\overset{4}{\cancel{9}},7\rangle = \langle 2, 3, 5, \underbrace{\textcolor{centrale_red}{1,9,8,}}_{\substack{\text{copied}\\\text{subsequence}}} 6, 4, 7\rangle
\end{equation}

Of course, we can do the same operation considering the parent $P_2$ in order to get two children. 

\paragraph{Cycle Crossover} The \emph{cycle crossover} aims to guarantee that each task of the offspring comes from the same exact position than one of the two parents. To do so, we start by taking the first task of the parent 1 and we copy it to the offspring. Then, we look at the same position of the second parent and we search the position of that task in the parent 1. We then continue the same process until we get back to the starting point (when we finish the cycle).

Let's take the example given in \cite{pwr_ea_task0_2026} where we have a first parent $P_1 = \langle 2, 7, 4, 3, 1 ,5, 6 ,9 ,8 \rangle$ and a second parent $P_2 = \langle 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9\rangle$. The first association of the cycle is therefore $1 \longleftrightarrow 2$. But $1$ in parent $1$ is in relation with $5$, and then $6$ and then finally $7$. By doing the same with other positions, we are able to get the full cycle $(1 \; 2 \; 7 \; 6 \; 5 \;1)$ and create the offspring (\figref{cycle_crossover_example}).

\begin{figure}[H]
    \centering
    \includegraphics[width=0.4\linewidth]{Zero_Project/illustration/cx_example.png}
    \caption{\textbf{Example of an CX crossover.} (Source: \cite{pwr_ea_task0_2026})}
    \label{fig:cycle_crossover_example}
\end{figure}


\subsubsection*{Elitism}

During the selection process, we can apply \emph{elitism} by copying the best individuals without any mutation. Therefore, we can have a parameter called "elitism" that represents the number of these copy. To do so, we first order the population and take the "elitism" size best ones and then put them directly in the next generation.
